Table 1
Rethinking, Intellectual Humility, and Scientific Thinking
1. Q: Why is the ability to rethink as important as the ability to think?
A: Show answer
Thinking helps people develop ideas, solve problems, and make decisions, but rethinking allows them to evaluate whether those ideas and decisions remain valid. Knowledge, technology, markets, and social conditions change, so a conclusion that was reasonable yesterday may no longer be appropriate today. People who rely exclusively on existing knowledge risk defending outdated assumptions instead of responding to new evidence. Rethinking involves examining beliefs, questioning established conclusions, and adjusting decisions when better information becomes available. It does not require rejecting everything previously learned. Rather, it requires distinguishing between knowledge that remains reliable and assumptions that need revision. The practical objective is to make decisions that are responsive to evidence rather than controlled by the need to appear consistently right.
Study instruction:
- Identify one important belief or decision you have revised because circumstances or evidence changed, and explain what prompted the revision.
Key point:
- Effective thinking includes the ability to reconsider conclusions.
- Intellectual flexibility helps people respond to changing conditions.
2. Q: What does rethinking mean, and how is it different from ordinary thinking?
A: Show answer
Ordinary thinking often applies existing knowledge, familiar assumptions, and established methods to a problem. Rethinking examines whether those assumptions, methods, and conclusions should still be trusted. For example, a manager might use an established marketing strategy because it previously generated strong sales. Ordinary thinking focuses on executing that strategy more efficiently, whereas rethinking asks whether customer behavior has changed and whether the original strategy still addresses current needs. Rethinking can involve collecting additional evidence, consulting people with different perspectives, running a small experiment, or abandoning an unsuccessful approach. It is especially useful when circumstances are uncertain or when previous success creates excessive confidence. The distinction is not between thinking correctly and thinking incorrectly; it is between using existing conclusions and examining whether those conclusions deserve to remain in place.
Study instruction:
- Select one established approach in your work and write down what ordinary thinking would improve and what rethinking would question.
Key point:
- Thinking applies existing understanding; rethinking tests whether that understanding remains sound.
- Reconsideration is particularly valuable when evidence or circumstances change.
3. Q: Why does the accelerating growth of knowledge make intellectual flexibility more necessary?
A: Show answer
When knowledge develops rapidly, the shelf life of particular facts, methods, and professional assumptions can become shorter. A person may become highly competent in a field while continuing to rely on information that no longer reflects current understanding. This creates a difference between accumulated expertise and current accuracy. Intellectual flexibility helps people identify when additional learning is necessary and when familiar conclusions should be updated. For example, a professional who relies on an old procedure without checking revised requirements may make avoidable errors even if that procedure was once appropriate. The correct response is not to abandon expertise but to maintain it through continuous learning, source verification, and periodic review. The faster a field changes, the more important it becomes to distinguish durable principles from temporary knowledge and context-dependent practices.
Study instruction:
- List three important facts, methods, or professional assumptions in your field and identify how you would verify that each remains current.
Key point:
- Expertise requires maintenance when knowledge and conditions change.
- Periodic verification prevents outdated information from becoming an unquestioned assumption.
4. Q: Why do people often become more committed to their beliefs instead of reconsidering them?
A: Show answer
Beliefs often become connected to personal identity, professional reputation, social relationships, and previous investments of time or money. When evidence challenges a belief, people may interpret the challenge as a threat rather than as an opportunity to learn. They can respond by defending the belief, dismissing contradictory information, or searching for additional arguments that support their existing position. Repeatedly rehearsing the same conclusion may make it feel increasingly familiar and convincing without making it more accurate. The problem becomes especially serious when admitting an error is perceived as a sign of incompetence or weakness. A more constructive approach separates the person from the belief: a conclusion can be revised without invalidating someone's worth or every previous achievement. This separation allows people to protect their capacity to learn rather than protecting every conclusion they have reached.
Study instruction:
- Recall a disagreement in which you became defensive and identify whether your reaction concerned the evidence, your identity, or your reputation.
Key point:
- Psychological attachment can make beliefs resistant to contrary evidence.
- Revising an idea does not require rejecting one's entire identity.
5. Q: Why should people seek second opinions about their own knowledge and decisions?
A: Show answer
People readily recognize that others can make mistakes, which is why they may seek a second medical opinion, consult another professional, or ask a colleague to review an important decision. However, they often assume that their own judgments deserve less scrutiny. A deliberate second-opinion habit corrects this asymmetry by treating personal conclusions as reviewable rather than automatically reliable. The process can involve asking an independent expert, checking primary evidence, comparing alternative explanations, or asking someone to identify weaknesses in the current plan. Independence matters because people who share the same assumptions may reproduce the same error. A second opinion is not necessarily correct simply because it differs from the first. Its value lies in expanding the evidence and perspectives considered before a decision becomes difficult or costly to reverse.
Study instruction:
- Choose one consequential decision and identify an independent person or source that could challenge your current conclusion constructively.
Key point:
- Personal judgments deserve the same scrutiny applied to other people's judgments.
- Independent review improves the range of evidence available for a decision.
6. Q: What are preacher, prosecutor, and politician modes of thinking?
A: Show answer
These three modes describe common ways people respond when their beliefs, arguments, or social standing are involved. In preacher mode, people defend deeply held values or convictions and try to persuade others that their position is correct. In prosecutor mode, they search for weaknesses in another person's argument and assemble reasons to demonstrate that the other person is wrong. In politician mode, they seek approval from an audience or influential group, adapting their position to maintain support. Each mode can serve a legitimate purpose in particular circumstances, but each can also interfere with learning. Preaching encourages defense, prosecuting encourages adversarial criticism, and politicking encourages approval-seeking. The central danger occurs when these reactions replace the examination of evidence. Scientific thinking offers a different orientation: investigating uncertainty, testing explanations, and adjusting conclusions according to what the evidence supports.
Study instruction:
- Describe one workplace disagreement and identify whether the participants were defending convictions, attacking opposing arguments, or seeking approval.
Key point:
- Preacher, prosecutor, and politician modes prioritize different forms of defense or persuasion.
- Recognizing these modes helps people choose a more evidence-oriented response.
7. Q: How can preacher mode prevent someone from recognizing a mistake?
A: Show answer
Preacher mode becomes problematic when a person treats a belief as something that must be protected rather than as a conclusion that may require examination. Instead of asking whether the belief is accurate, the person searches for reasons to reinforce it and may interpret criticism as an attack on important values. This response can prevent useful feedback from being considered, particularly when the belief is associated with professional pride or past success. For example, a department head may continue defending a reporting process because they designed it, even after repeated errors indicate that it needs improvement. The individual may concentrate on explaining why the original design made sense rather than investigating why it no longer works. A better response is to preserve the underlying objective while remaining willing to revise the method used to achieve it.
Study instruction:
- Identify a belief you strongly defend and write two questions that would help you test it rather than justify it.
Key point:
- Defending a conviction can distract from evaluating its accuracy.
- Protecting an objective does not require protecting every method used to achieve it.
8. Q: How does prosecutor mode interfere with learning and problem-solving?
A: Show answer
Prosecutor mode directs attention toward finding weaknesses in another person's reasoning. This can be useful when evaluating arguments, identifying errors, or investigating misconduct. It becomes counterproductive when the primary goal is to defeat the other person rather than understand the issue. People may concentrate on minor inconsistencies, disregard evidence supporting the opposing position, and construct increasingly elaborate arguments for their original conclusion. As a result, disagreement becomes a contest of persuasion rather than a collaborative search for accuracy. In a workplace, this can discourage colleagues from reporting risks or presenting alternative solutions. A more productive approach asks which parts of the opposing argument are supported, which parts are weak, and whether any evidence should change the original assessment. Criticizing an argument is most useful when it improves the shared understanding of the problem.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite one critical response to an opposing opinion as three evidence-focused questions that both parties could investigate.
Key point:
- Winning an argument is different from discovering an accurate explanation.
- Constructive criticism should improve understanding rather than merely discredit another person.
9. Q: How can politician mode distort a person's judgment?
A: Show answer
Politician mode emphasizes gaining approval from an audience, maintaining support, or avoiding social rejection. A person may choose a position because it satisfies colleagues, family members, supervisors, or influential stakeholders rather than because it has the strongest supporting evidence. This can lead to selective disclosure, reluctance to acknowledge unfavorable results, and resistance to proposals that might be unpopular. In an organization, a manager might continue funding a weak project because influential sponsors support it, even though performance indicators show that the project is unlikely to succeed. The individual is effectively optimizing social acceptance rather than the quality of the decision. To reduce this risk, decision-makers should establish evaluation criteria before receiving pressure from interested parties, document the evidence behind conclusions, and create opportunities for dissenting views to be considered without personal retaliation.
Study instruction:
- Examine a decision influenced by seniority or group opinion and distinguish the evidence-based reasons from the reasons related to approval.
Key point:
- Social approval can influence judgments independently of evidence.
- Transparent criteria and protected dissent can reduce approval-driven decisions.
10. Q: What distinguishes scientific thinking from preacher, prosecutor, and politician thinking?
A: Show answer
Scientific thinking begins with uncertainty and treats explanations as ideas that can be tested. Rather than assuming that an existing belief is correct, a scientific thinker asks what evidence supports it, what evidence might contradict it, and how alternative explanations compare. Preacher mode tends to defend convictions, prosecutor mode tends to challenge opponents, and politician mode tends to seek approval. Scientific thinking instead prioritizes learning from observations and revising conclusions when the evidence warrants it. This approach does not eliminate judgment or guarantee perfect accuracy. Results may be incomplete, measurements may be flawed, and experiments may be poorly designed. Its advantage is that it makes the process of correcting errors explicit. People can apply this approach in business, relationships, education, and ordinary decision-making without being professional scientists.
Study instruction:
- Select a problem and outline the current explanation, alternative explanations, observable evidence, and conditions that would cause you to change your conclusion.
Key point:
- Scientific thinking treats beliefs as testable rather than sacred or politically convenient.
- Its purpose is to improve understanding through evidence and revision.
11. Q: What is intellectual humility, and why is it essential to rethinking?
A: Show answer
Intellectual humility is the recognition that personal knowledge and judgment have limitations. It does not mean assuming that every opinion is equally valid or denying genuine expertise. Instead, it means understanding the difference between what is known, what is uncertain, and what remains unexplored. A person with intellectual humility can explain the reasons for a current conclusion while acknowledging that additional evidence could change it. This attitude creates room for questions, external review, and correction. Without it, people may interpret expertise as proof that their judgments are universally reliable. In practical settings, intellectual humility encourages professionals to consult specialists outside their own fields, verify assumptions, and disclose uncertainty when reporting to management. It also makes learning more efficient because people can identify specific knowledge gaps rather than concealing them behind confidence.
Study instruction:
- Write down three areas in which you have limited knowledge and identify one reliable source or person from whom you could learn more.
Key point:
- Intellectual humility acknowledges limitations without abandoning evidence-based judgment.
- Recognizing knowledge gaps is a starting point for improvement.
12. Q: How should uncertainty be treated when making an important decision?
A: Show answer
Uncertainty should be treated as information about the limits of the current analysis, not automatically as evidence of incompetence. When important facts are missing, a decision-maker can distinguish confirmed information from estimates, assumptions, and unresolved questions. The next step is to determine which uncertainties could materially affect the outcome and what information would help reduce them. Sometimes it is appropriate to delay a decision while gathering evidence. In other situations, waiting is more costly than acting, so the decision-maker should choose a reversible option and establish conditions for reassessment. The goal is not to eliminate all uncertainty before acting. It is to make uncertainty visible and manage its consequences deliberately. This approach also improves communication because others can understand the confidence level of a recommendation and what developments could change it.
Study instruction:
- For an upcoming decision, separate established facts, assumptions, unknowns, and the information needed before final approval.
Key point:
- Clear uncertainty statements improve decision quality and communication.
- High-impact unknowns should receive attention before low-impact details.
13. Q: What does it mean to treat a strategy as a hypothesis rather than a fact?
A: Show answer
Treating a strategy as a hypothesis means recognizing that the expected outcome is a prediction to be tested, not a guarantee. A business might believe that customers prefer a lower-priced product, that a new service will attract more users, or that a particular distribution method will increase sales. Each proposition can be translated into an observable prediction. The organization can then collect customer feedback, measure behavior, and compare actual results with the expected outcome. If the evidence contradicts the hypothesis, the appropriate response is to investigate the discrepancy and consider revising the strategy. This does not mean changing direction after every disappointing result. Measurements may be noisy, time frames may be inadequate, or implementation may be faulty. The important principle is to specify what success would look like and establish how results will inform the next decision.
Study instruction:
- Convert one current project assumption into a testable prediction with a measurable outcome and a review date.
Key point:
- A strategy should be evaluated against observable results.
- Testing assumptions is more informative than defending plans indefinitely.
14. Q: How can experiments improve everyday decisions without requiring scientific equipment or specialized training?
A: Show answer
An experiment is a deliberate test designed to learn whether an assumption holds under specified conditions. Everyday experiments can be simple: testing two versions of a presentation, trying a revised meeting format with one team, or offering a small group of customers a prototype before a full launch. The essential features are a clear question, a defined outcome, an appropriate comparison where feasible, and a willingness to learn from the result. Experiments reduce dependence on intuition because they create opportunities to observe how people actually respond. They are especially valuable when the cost of a small test is much lower than the cost of a full commitment. However, a small experiment may not represent every customer or situation, so its limits should be considered before generalizing the findings.
Study instruction:
- Design a low-cost test for one assumption in your work and specify the success measure, observation period, and limitations.
Key point:
- Small experiments can test assumptions before major resources are committed.
- Results must be interpreted in light of the experiment's scope and limitations.
15. Q: What does the experience of BlackBerry illustrate about successful products and the danger of resisting change?
A: Show answer
BlackBerry's early success came from solving a real communication problem through convenient mobile email, a recognizable keyboard, and a product experience valued by many users. Its later decline illustrates how strengths that create early success can become limitations when customer expectations and competitive conditions change. Leadership remained strongly attached to features that had made the device popular and was reluctant to fully test alternatives associated with broader smartphone capabilities. This reduced the organization's ability to evaluate emerging opportunities objectively. The lesson is not that a successful product should be abandoned whenever a competitor introduces a new feature. It is that past success does not prove that a product's advantages will remain decisive. Organizations must periodically reexamine customer needs, competitor capabilities, technological developments, and assumptions about the future market.
Study instruction:
- Identify a product or process that once performed well and list the changed conditions that might require its redesign.
Key point:
- Previous success is evidence of past effectiveness, not a guarantee of future relevance.
- Leaders must keep testing whether established strengths still meet current needs.
16. Q: Why can expertise and intelligence sometimes make rethinking more difficult?
A: Show answer
Expertise and intelligence provide valuable resources for solving problems, recognizing patterns, and analyzing information. However, they do not automatically create a willingness to revise beliefs. Highly capable people may use their reasoning skills to defend established positions, explain away contradictions, or construct sophisticated justifications for choices they already prefer. Greater familiarity with a subject can also increase confidence in the assumptions underlying one's expertise. The resulting problem is not intelligence itself but the direction in which it is applied. Reasoning can be used to investigate whether a conclusion is correct or to protect the conclusion from challenge. Effective experts therefore need both analytical ability and intellectual flexibility. They should remain aware that knowledge in one field does not guarantee sound judgment in every field, particularly when unfamiliar evidence conflicts with their expectations or interests.
Study instruction:
- Review a decision in which expertise influenced your confidence and identify evidence that you might have discounted because it challenged your initial judgment.
Key point:
- Reasoning ability does not automatically produce open-mindedness.
- Expertise is most useful when combined with a willingness to test assumptions.
17. Q: What is confirmation bias, and how does it influence decisions?
A: Show answer
Confirmation bias is the tendency to notice, interpret, or seek information in ways that favor an existing belief. A person who expects a new product to succeed may focus on positive customer comments while overlooking complaints, low repeat purchases, or weak sales figures. The bias can operate even when the person has no intention of being misleading. Attention itself is selective, and familiar explanations can make supporting evidence easier to recognize than contradictory evidence. Confirmation bias becomes especially damaging when decisions involve financial commitments, professional identity, or strong social loyalties. To counter it, decision-makers should deliberately look for counterexamples, examine unfavorable results, and ask what evidence would weaken their current position. An independent reviewer can also help identify information that the original decision-maker has overlooked or interpreted too favorably.
Study instruction:
- Take a belief you currently hold and document three pieces of potentially contradictory evidence that would be important to investigate.
Key point:
- Confirmation bias makes supportive information easier to notice and accept.
- Deliberately searching for contrary evidence improves the quality of evaluation.
18. Q: What is desirability bias, and how does it differ from confirmation bias?
A: Show answer
Desirability bias occurs when people are more likely to accept a conclusion because they want it to be true. Confirmation bias concerns the tendency to favor information consistent with existing beliefs, while desirability bias emphasizes the influence of preferred outcomes. The two can reinforce each other. An investor who wants an investment to produce exceptional returns may interpret ambiguous information optimistically and pay less attention to warning signs. A manager who strongly wants a project to succeed may accept encouraging forecasts while minimizing disappointing performance. The practical danger is that preferences begin to influence the interpretation of evidence. A useful safeguard is to distinguish what a decision-maker hopes will happen from what the available information actually demonstrates. Decisions should be evaluated against independently defined criteria rather than the emotional appeal of the desired result.
Study instruction:
- Write down one outcome you strongly want and identify what evidence would be needed to establish that it is actually likely.
Key point:
- Desirability bias allows preferences to influence judgments about reality.
- Separating desired outcomes from supporting evidence reduces motivated reasoning.
19. Q: What is the blind spot that occurs when people believe they are less biased than others?
A: Show answer
People can recognize common biases in other individuals while assuming that their own judgments are relatively objective. This creates a bias blind spot: awareness of bias in general does not necessarily translate into awareness of bias in oneself. The problem is particularly difficult because people experience their own reasoning from the inside. They can recall the reasons they considered but may not notice information they ignored, assumptions they accepted, or emotions that influenced their attention. High confidence and strong analytical ability do not automatically eliminate these weaknesses. Practical safeguards include documenting assumptions before outcomes are known, inviting independent criticism, comparing predictions with results, and examining decisions that turned out differently than expected. The objective is not to claim complete neutrality but to develop processes that can reveal errors even when personal introspection cannot.
Study instruction:
- Identify a recurring judgment you make about other people and consider whether you apply the same standard to yourself.
Key point:
- Recognizing bias in others does not guarantee awareness of one's own bias.
- Independent review and documented criteria help reveal blind spots.
20. Q: How can analytical ability become a liability when evidence challenges someone's ideology?
A: Show answer
Analytical ability can become counterproductive when it is used selectively to defend an ideological position. A person may carefully examine numerical evidence when it supports their views but apply less rigorous scrutiny when it contradicts them. Strong analytical skills can then produce elaborate rationalizations instead of better conclusions. The danger is not that mathematics or logic becomes inherently unreliable, but that motivated reasoning determines which methods are used and how results are interpreted. This pattern can appear in politics, business, professional debates, and financial decisions. A useful safeguard is to apply consistent analytical standards regardless of the expected conclusion. Decision-makers can also evaluate evidence without initially identifying which side it supports, where practical, and ask independent reviewers to assess the reasoning separately from the preferred outcome.
Study instruction:
- Choose a controversial claim and specify one consistent evidence-evaluation method that you would apply whether the result supported or opposed your position.
Key point:
- Analytical sophistication cannot compensate for selectively applied reasoning.
- Consistent evaluation standards reduce the influence of ideological preferences.
21. Q: Why was Stephen Greenspan's investment in the Madoff fund a useful example of failed rethinking?
A: Show answer
Stephen Greenspan invested a substantial portion of his retirement savings in a fund that later collapsed as part of Bernie Madoff's Ponzi scheme. His decision was influenced by encouraging reports from people he trusted, a financial adviser who believed in the investment, and his dismissal of a skeptical friend's warning. These factors created a combination of social trust, favorable impressions, and selective evaluation of risk. The case demonstrates that expertise in a subject does not guarantee good judgment when personal relationships and desired outcomes influence decisions. It also illustrates why reported historical returns are insufficient by themselves to establish that an investment is legitimate. A better approach would have required independent verification of the investment strategy, custody arrangements, financial records, regulatory status, and risks before committing a large amount of savings.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a major investment decision and list the independent checks that should be completed before relying on reputation or reported performance.
Key point:
- Trust and positive historical results do not replace independent verification.
- Large financial commitments require evidence proportionate to the potential loss.
22. Q: How should an investor evaluate an opportunity when trusted people recommend it but another person raises concerns?
A: Show answer
The investor should separate the recommendation, the evidence, the source's credibility, and the investment's actual risk. Begin by identifying how the investment generates returns, what could cause losses, how assets are held, and whether independent documentation supports the reported results. Verify relevant registrations and regulatory disclosures where applicable, examine fees and withdrawal restrictions, and seek qualified independent advice when necessary. Avoid treating friendship, social proof, or an adviser's personal investment as conclusive proof of safety. At the same time, skepticism should be applied systematically rather than accepted merely because it sounds cautious. The goal is to compare competing claims using evidence that can be independently checked. Where important facts remain unverifiable, reducing the commitment or declining the opportunity may be more prudent than relying on reassurance.
Study instruction:
- Create a due-diligence checklist for a hypothetical investment and separate verifiable facts, unsupported claims, and unresolved risks.
Key point:
- Recommendations require independent verification before substantial commitments.
- Unverifiable claims should be treated as unresolved risks, not as established facts.
23. Q: How can personal relationships interfere with evidence-based decisions?
A: Show answer
Relationships can influence decisions because people want to preserve trust, avoid embarrassment, and maintain approval from those they value. A recommendation from a family friend or respected colleague may receive less scrutiny than an identical recommendation from a stranger. This can cause a decision-maker to confuse the credibility of a relationship with the quality of the underlying evidence. The risk is particularly high when refusing a proposal may create social discomfort or when agreeing to it appears to demonstrate loyalty. A disciplined decision process separates the interpersonal relationship from the substantive merits of the proposal. The decision-maker can acknowledge the relationship respectfully while applying the same standards, documentation, and due diligence used for other opportunities. This protects both decision quality and relationships by making the evaluation process transparent rather than personal.
Study instruction:
- Think of a situation where you accepted advice from someone familiar and identify the independent checks you would apply to the same advice from an unfamiliar person.
Key point:
- Familiarity and trust should not replace substantive evaluation.
- Consistent decision criteria help manage social pressure fairly.
24. Q: Why is testing a small investment or initiative often preferable to making a large commitment immediately?
A: Show answer
A small initial test can produce useful information while limiting the cost of being wrong. Instead of committing a large share of savings to an unfamiliar investment or deploying a new business model throughout an organization, a decision-maker can begin with an amount or scope that is manageable. The test should have a clear objective, a defined observation period, and criteria for deciding whether to continue, modify, or stop. This approach is valuable when uncertainty is high and additional evidence can be obtained before the decision becomes difficult to reverse. However, small tests do not automatically establish that a larger commitment will be safe or successful. Conditions may change with scale, and some risks may only emerge over time. The results should therefore inform a larger decision rather than replace its due diligence.
Study instruction:
- Design a pilot for a proposed project and specify the maximum initial commitment, the evidence to collect, and the criteria for expanding or stopping it.
Key point:
- Small, well-designed tests can reduce uncertainty before large commitments.
- Scaling should depend on evidence and risks that remain after the pilot.
25. Q: What did the startup experiment described in the chapter suggest about scientific thinking in entrepreneurship?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a study in which founders received similar entrepreneurship training, but one group was specifically encouraged to apply scientific thinking. Participants in that group were taught to treat strategies as theories, customer interviews as ways to develop hypotheses, and prototypes as experiments. The chapter reports that these startups generated substantially more revenue than the comparison group and changed direction more frequently when their assumptions were not supported. The result illustrates how systematic testing can help founders respond to market information rather than remain committed to an original plan. However, the reported finding should not be interpreted as proof that one method guarantees success for every business. The study's setting, participants, intervention, and observation period matter. Its broader lesson is that assumptions should be tested and business decisions should respond to what customers and actual performance reveal.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a startup could convert a business strategy into a hypothesis and define the customer evidence needed to assess it.
Key point:
- Scientific thinking can make entrepreneurship more adaptive and evidence-oriented.
- Study results should be interpreted within the limits of their design and context.
26. Q: Why is the willingness to pivot an important feature of evidence-based entrepreneurship?
A: Show answer
A pivot involves making a meaningful change to a business strategy, product, customer group, or operating approach in response to what has been learned. It is useful when testing reveals that the original assumptions do not adequately explain customer behavior or commercial performance. A founder who treats the first business plan as a commitment to defend may continue spending resources despite repeated evidence of weak demand. A founder who treats the plan as a hypothesis can investigate the cause of poor results and test an alternative. Pivoting should not mean changing direction randomly whenever an early result disappoints. The entrepreneur must distinguish between a flawed assumption, temporary variation, inadequate execution, and insufficient data. A carefully evaluated pivot preserves useful learning while changing the elements that evidence suggests are not working.
Study instruction:
- Develop a decision rule for when a project should continue as planned, be modified, or be stopped based on measurable results.
Key point:
- A pivot is a response to learning, not an automatic reaction to every setback.
- Clear decision criteria help distinguish necessary adaptation from unfocused change.
27. Q: Why can decisiveness be overrated when leaders face uncertainty?
A: Show answer
Decisiveness is valuable when action is needed, but excessive emphasis on appearing certain can discourage information gathering and revision. Leaders may rush toward a visible decision because employees and stakeholders expect confidence, even when the available evidence is incomplete. This creates the danger of confusing speed with judgment. A more effective leader can make timely decisions while acknowledging uncertainty, defining assumptions, and preserving opportunities to adjust. For reversible decisions, a small experiment or temporary arrangement may provide better information than a premature commitment. Irreversible decisions may require deeper analysis and more deliberate review. The appropriate pace depends on urgency, potential consequences, cost of delay, and reversibility. Confidence should reflect the quality of the evidence, not a desire to project authority.
Study instruction:
- Review one decision that requires timely action and explain which parts can be tested or reversed before a permanent commitment is made.
Key point:
- Speed and certainty are not reliable substitutes for decision quality.
- Appropriate caution depends on urgency, risk, evidence, and reversibility.
28. Q: How can scientific thinking be applied by professionals who are not scientists?
A: Show answer
Scientific thinking is a method of approaching questions, not a job title. A finance professional can test whether a reconciliation procedure reduces recurring errors. A teacher can compare learning outcomes under different instructional methods. A manager can examine whether a meeting redesign improves the speed of decisions. In each case, the person identifies an assumption, develops a prediction, collects relevant observations, and assesses whether the results support the prediction. The approach also requires attention to measurement quality and alternative explanations. A successful outcome does not necessarily prove that the proposed method caused the improvement, especially if other conditions changed simultaneously. Non-scientists can nevertheless benefit from treating plans as testable, documenting findings, and distinguishing observations from interpretations. The central skill is learning systematically from experience rather than relying exclusively on intuition or habit.
Study instruction:
- Select a recurring workplace problem and design a simple test with a measurable outcome and a way to compare performance before and after the change.
Key point:
- Scientific thinking is applicable to ordinary work and personal decisions.
- Good tests require clear questions, reliable observations, and careful interpretation.
29. Q: How did Mike Lazaridis's approach to developing BlackBerry differ from his later approach to leading the company?
A: Show answer
In developing BlackBerry, Lazaridis examined shortcomings in existing wireless email devices and explored alternative technical solutions. He encouraged engineers to investigate possibilities such as easier thumb typing, synchronized email, and more effective message delivery. This approach resembled scientific problem-solving because competing design ideas could be tested and refined. As the smartphone market developed, however, the chapter describes how Lazaridis remained strongly attached to the product features that had originally made BlackBerry successful. Concerns about touchscreen interfaces, internet browsing, and broader smartphone capabilities were not always subjected to sufficiently open testing. The contrast shows that a person can use flexible thinking in one role and become resistant to reconsideration in another. Technical ingenuity does not automatically produce strategic flexibility, especially when decisions become intertwined with ownership, status, and a successful personal achievement.
Study instruction:
- Compare the development of a successful product with its subsequent strategic management and identify where assumptions should be reassessed.
Key point:
- A person can be innovative in one context and inflexible in another.
- Leadership requires continuing to test assumptions after initial success.
30. Q: How can an organization prevent its previous successes from becoming obstacles to innovation?
A: Show answer
Previous success can create incentives to preserve the strategies, products, and processes associated with it. Employees may fear losing resources, status, or credibility if a successful approach is questioned. An organization can reduce this risk by regularly reviewing assumptions, soliciting customer feedback, testing alternatives, and distinguishing the purpose of a product from the features currently used to deliver it. Leaders should ask which established practices remain essential and which reflect conditions that may have changed. They can also set aside resources for controlled experiments so that innovation does not depend entirely on abandoning current operations. Successful organizations need continuity where it preserves genuine value and change where evidence indicates that adaptation is necessary. The objective is not novelty for its own sake, but maintaining the ability to respond when the environment changes.
Study instruction:
- Identify one successful organizational practice and document what should be preserved, what could change, and how an alternative would be tested.
Key point:
- Past performance should inform innovation without preventing it.
- Protecting core objectives can coexist with changing products and methods.
31. Q: How can leaders encourage employees to challenge existing strategies without creating unnecessary instability?
A: Show answer
Leaders can establish a structured process for questioning important assumptions rather than allowing every disagreement to trigger an immediate operational change. Employees should be able to present evidence, identify risks, propose alternatives, and recommend small tests. Management can then evaluate proposals against agreed criteria, consider their consequences, and determine whether a pilot or broader revision is justified. This separates the right to question a strategy from the authority to change it without approval. It also prevents disagreement from becoming a personal contest. Leaders should explain why a decision is retained or revised so that employees understand how evidence influenced the outcome. When practical, documenting the original prediction and subsequent results makes the learning process visible and helps the organization improve its judgment over time.
Study instruction:
- Draft a team review process that allows employees to challenge assumptions while preserving clear ownership and approval responsibilities.
Key point:
- Constructive challenge should be encouraged within a disciplined decision process.
- Evidence-based review can support adaptation without creating uncontrolled changes.
32. Q: How did Steve Jobs's response to the proposed iPhone illustrate the value of preserving continuity during change?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes how Jobs initially opposed the idea of developing a phone, partly because he was concerned about risks to Apple's existing business and the constraints imposed by mobile carriers. Members of his team worked to change his view by framing the proposal as an extension of Apple's identity rather than a complete rejection of it. They argued that Apple could remain a computer-oriented company while exploring a new way to bring computing capabilities into people's daily lives. This framing helped address the concern that the proposed product would undermine what the organization already represented. The example illustrates that rethinking does not always require abandoning identity or past achievements. Sometimes people become more willing to change when they understand which core principles will remain stable while strategies evolve.
Study instruction:
- Develop a change proposal that identifies the essential elements to preserve and the methods that need to evolve.
Key point:
- Continuity can make significant change easier to evaluate and accept.
- A stable purpose does not require a permanently fixed strategy.
33. Q: Why can emphasizing continuity be more persuasive than presenting change as a complete break with the past?
A: Show answer
People may resist change when they believe it threatens their identity, expertise, or sense of belonging. A proposal framed as abandoning everything familiar can intensify these concerns, even when the proposed change offers real benefits. Emphasizing continuity clarifies what remains valuable while explaining which methods, assumptions, or activities must evolve. For example, a company could present digital transformation as a way to serve customers more effectively rather than as a repudiation of its entire operating history. This approach is not an excuse to conceal the scale of a change or promise that nothing important will be lost. Instead, it establishes an honest distinction between enduring objectives and revisable practices. The most persuasive proposal explains why the change is needed, what will remain, what will differ, and how the effects will be evaluated.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a change proposal so that it clearly distinguishes the organization's enduring purpose from the practices that need revision.
Key point:
- Change can be presented as an evolution of purpose rather than an attack on identity.
- Credible change communication must describe both continuity and genuine differences.
34. Q: How should customer feedback influence product and business decisions?
A: Show answer
Customer feedback should be treated as evidence about user needs, experiences, and potential problems, while recognizing that individual comments do not always represent broader demand. Businesses can combine interviews with behavioral data, retention measures, sales results, usability testing, and observations of how customers actually use a product. A customer may say that a feature is attractive without ultimately using it or paying for it. Conversely, users may struggle to articulate a need that becomes clear through observation or experimentation. Decision-makers should therefore investigate patterns across multiple sources, consider who provided the feedback, and examine whether the evidence supports the original assumption. When results are mixed, additional targeted tests may be more informative than an immediate major redesign. The objective is to let customer evidence influence strategy without treating every comment as a definitive instruction.
Study instruction:
- Create a feedback-analysis plan that combines customer interviews with at least one behavioral or performance measure.
Key point:
- Customer opinions are valuable but should be evaluated alongside observed behavior.
- Multiple forms of evidence improve the basis for product decisions.
35. Q: How can a person distinguish a durable principle from an outdated assumption?
A: Show answer
A durable principle continues to provide a useful explanation across changing circumstances, while an assumption may depend on temporary conditions that no longer exist. The distinction cannot always be determined in advance, so both should be examined. Ask what evidence supports the principle, what conditions must hold for it to apply, and whether those conditions remain present. For example, a preference for clear communication may remain valuable, while the belief that one specific communication technology is the best way to achieve it may become outdated. This separation prevents people from rejecting an important objective merely because a familiar method has stopped working. It also prevents them from retaining a method simply because it was historically associated with success. Useful rethinking preserves what the evidence continues to support and modifies what no longer fits the situation.
Study instruction:
- Select a familiar rule or belief and separate its underlying objective from the particular circumstances or method on which it depends.
Key point:
- Durable principles and context-dependent assumptions should not be treated as identical.
- Reconsidering a method can preserve the objective it was designed to achieve.
36. Q: How can people identify the evidence that would change their minds before making a decision?
A: Show answer
Before committing to a conclusion, a person can specify what observations would support it, what observations would weaken it, and what level of evidence would justify further action. This helps prevent decision criteria from changing merely to protect a preferred outcome. For instance, a project owner might establish performance targets and a review date before launching a pilot. If the results fail to meet the predefined criteria, the owner must investigate the cause rather than automatically explaining away the outcome. The criteria should remain open to legitimate revision when unexpected conditions or measurement problems emerge, but changes must be documented and justified. The purpose is to establish a fair method of learning, not to create inflexible rules that ignore relevant new information. Predefined evidence standards make decisions easier to review and explain afterward.
Study instruction:
- For a current proposal, define two observations that would support the proposal and two that would cause you to reconsider it.
Key point:
- Deciding in advance what could change your mind reduces retrospective rationalization.
- Evaluation criteria should be documented and revised only for defensible reasons.
37. Q: What is active open-mindedness, and how does it differ from simply being willing to listen?
A: Show answer
Passive openness means being willing to hear another opinion. Active open-mindedness goes further by deliberately searching for reasons that one's own view might be mistaken. A person can listen politely to a different perspective yet leave the conversation with unchanged assumptions because no genuine evaluation has occurred. Active open-mindedness requires asking questions, investigating contradictory evidence, considering alternative explanations, and revising conclusions when the evidence supports doing so. It does not mean treating all opinions as equally reliable or abandoning standards of proof. A claim supported by stronger evidence should receive greater weight than one based on speculation. Active openness involves both intellectual curiosity and disciplined evaluation, allowing people to learn from disagreement without automatically accepting or rejecting what they hear.
Study instruction:
- Before your next important discussion, prepare questions that could reveal weaknesses in your own position rather than only questions that challenge others.
Key point:
- Open-mindedness requires actively investigating the possibility of being wrong.
- Alternative views should be evaluated by the strength of their evidence.
38. Q: What is the rethinking cycle, and how does it support continuous learning?
A: Show answer
The rethinking cycle begins with intellectual humility, which allows a person to recognize limits in their understanding. That recognition creates room for doubt about whether the current explanation is complete. Doubt encourages curiosity about missing information, alternative interpretations, and possible errors. Investigation then produces new information that can support or challenge the original belief. The resulting learning creates an opportunity to refine the conclusion while recognizing that additional uncertainty may remain. This is a continuing process rather than a one-time correction. Each discovery can reveal new questions and help a person identify further gaps in knowledge. The cycle is useful because it treats uncertainty as a prompt for inquiry instead of a condition that must be concealed. Over time, it encourages decisions that are more adaptable, transparent, and responsive to evidence.
Study instruction:
- Apply the four stages of humility, doubt, curiosity, and discovery to a problem you recently investigated.
Key point:
- Humility creates room for doubt, which motivates curiosity and discovery.
- Learning is iterative because each answer can reveal new questions.
39. Q: How does the overconfidence cycle undermine learning and judgment?
A: Show answer
An overconfidence cycle can begin when a person takes pride in a belief or achievement and becomes excessively certain that their understanding is correct. That certainty discourages the recognition of knowledge gaps and makes opposing arguments seem defective. The person may then search mainly for supporting evidence and seek approval from people who already agree. Positive feedback strengthens confidence even if the available evidence is incomplete or unfavorable information is being ignored. As this process continues, the person becomes less likely to test assumptions or investigate risks. The cycle can be particularly damaging after repeated success because praise and favorable outcomes may create a false impression that the existing approach is permanently reliable. Breaking the cycle requires deliberate questioning, independent review, and transparent comparison between predicted and actual results.
Study instruction:
- Identify signs of excessive confidence in a project or decision and develop one control that would expose unsupported assumptions.
Key point:
- Pride and selective validation can reinforce unsupported certainty.
- Independent evidence and systematic review can interrupt the overconfidence cycle.
40. Q: How can people avoid becoming complacent after repeated success?
A: Show answer
Repeated success can encourage people to rely on familiar methods, assume their judgment is reliable in new situations, and discount emerging warnings. This complacency becomes dangerous when the environment changes or when the original achievements depended on circumstances that no longer apply. A constructive response is to continue testing important assumptions even when current performance appears satisfactory. Leaders can review unexpected outcomes, examine near misses, compare their methods with credible alternatives, and ask whether current success is masking weaknesses. It is also useful to separate success achieved through a sound process from success that may depend partly on favorable conditions or chance. The purpose is not to undermine confidence unnecessarily but to keep confidence connected to current evidence. Continued review helps preserve effective methods while identifying changes that may be needed before performance declines.
Study instruction:
- Review a successful project and distinguish the practices that contributed to the result from assumptions that remain untested.
Key point:
- Success should trigger continued evaluation rather than permanent confidence in the existing approach.
- Reviewing assumptions while performance is strong can expose risks early.
41. Q: Why is cognitive flexibility important for effective leaders?
A: Show answer
Cognitive flexibility is the ability to consider different perspectives, move between alternative explanations, and revise one's approach when circumstances require it. Leaders need this capacity because organizational problems rarely remain constant and no single method works in every situation. A leader may need to preserve a long-term objective while changing operational priorities, seek expert advice outside their experience, or abandon an assumption supported by earlier results. Excessive rigidity can cause leaders to overlook opportunities, dismiss employee warnings, and continue ineffective policies. Excessive change without evidence can also undermine trust and execution. Effective flexibility therefore combines openness to alternatives with disciplined evaluation and clear communication. Leaders should explain the evidence behind revisions, identify the implications for staff, and distinguish necessary adaptation from changes that are merely fashionable or politically convenient.
Study instruction:
- Describe a leadership decision that would benefit from considering at least three plausible explanations or courses of action.
Key point:
- Cognitive flexibility allows leaders to adapt while maintaining clear objectives.
- Good leadership combines openness with evidence, accountability, and consistent communication.
42. Q: How can intellectual curiosity improve the quality of leadership decisions?
A: Show answer
Intellectual curiosity motivates leaders to investigate unfamiliar subjects, listen to informed disagreement, and reconsider established policies. It reduces the risk that a leader's knowledge remains confined to the areas in which they already have experience. A curious leader may learn from developments in technology, behavioral science, economics, or other disciplines that offer useful insights into organizational challenges. Curiosity also helps leaders ask better questions before committing resources. However, curiosity alone is not enough: new information needs to be evaluated for relevance, reliability, and practical consequences. Leaders should not adopt an idea simply because it is novel or comes from a prestigious source. The objective is to use curiosity to broaden the evidence base and then make decisions through careful reasoning, appropriate consultation, and review of actual results.
Study instruction:
- Identify one unfamiliar field that could inform a current work challenge and define how its knowledge could be evaluated before being applied.
Key point:
- Curiosity broadens the information and perspectives available to decision-makers.
- New ideas require critical evaluation before they become organizational practice.
43. Q: What can the chapter's discussion of great scientists, artists, and presidents teach us about intellectual flexibility?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that intellectual flexibility and openness to learning can contribute to exceptional performance across different fields. Its examples suggest that valuable contributors are not necessarily those who remain committed to one approach under every circumstance. Instead, they can explore competing perspectives, consider unfamiliar knowledge, and revise plans as new information emerges. In leadership, this means treating policies and strategies as approaches to be evaluated rather than as statements of personal identity. In creative work, it means being willing to explore different possibilities and reconsider initial designs. These examples do not establish that openness alone guarantees greatness, because achievement also depends on knowledge, execution, opportunities, and other factors. They highlight a useful capability: the ability to adapt one's thinking to the demands of a particular problem without being trapped by established habits.
Study instruction:
- Identify a successful leader or creator and examine how they responded to new evidence, alternative perspectives, or changing conditions.
Key point:
- Intellectual flexibility can support performance across professional and creative disciplines.
- Openness is a valuable capability, but it does not independently guarantee success.
44. Q: How can educators prevent outdated assumptions from remaining embedded in teaching materials?
A: Show answer
Educational institutions can review curricula, textbooks, and teaching practices against current research and established learning objectives. A statement that was once accepted may be revised as new discoveries emerge, and instructional material should reflect meaningful changes in understanding. This requires identifying the sources behind important claims, assigning responsibility for periodic review, and documenting decisions about revisions. Updates should not be made simply because a claim is new or popular. Educators need to examine the quality of the evidence, distinguish settled findings from active debate, and communicate uncertainty appropriately. Outdated material may persist because of administrative delays, limited budgets, or the effort required to change established curricula. A systematic review process makes updating more manageable and helps students understand that knowledge can develop while rigorous standards for evidence remain important.
Study instruction:
- Select one topic from an established curriculum and outline how its factual claims and supporting sources could be reviewed for currency and reliability.
Key point:
- Educational content should be updated when credible evidence materially changes established understanding.
- Systematic review reduces the persistence of obsolete claims.
45. Q: How can teams turn disagreement into a process of discovery rather than a contest of opinions?
A: Show answer
Teams can frame disagreement around a question that can be investigated rather than around which person should prevail. Participants should state their assumptions, explain the evidence supporting their conclusions, and identify what information could change their views. A team can then compare competing explanations, agree on a useful test, and examine the result together. This process reduces the temptation to rely on status, personal loyalty, or rhetorical confidence as substitutes for evidence. It also helps preserve constructive working relationships because participants can change their views without being treated as defeated. Not every disagreement can be resolved experimentally, and some decisions require judgment when evidence is incomplete. Even in those circumstances, documenting the alternatives considered and the reasons for the final choice makes the process more transparent and easier to revisit.
Study instruction:
- Design a structured discussion for a disputed workplace issue, including assumptions, evidence, alternative explanations, and a decision rule.
Key point:
- Productive disagreement focuses on testing explanations rather than defeating colleagues.
- Shared criteria and documented evidence improve the quality of group decisions.
46. Q: How should an individual respond when new evidence contradicts an established belief?
A: Show answer
The individual should first determine whether the evidence is credible, relevant, and strong enough to challenge the existing conclusion. This involves checking the source, understanding the methods used, considering alternative explanations, and comparing the new information with the evidence supporting the original belief. If the contradiction is substantial, the next step is to revise the conclusion, narrow the circumstances in which it applies, or acknowledge that the available information is insufficient. A person should also consider whether previous decisions based on the belief require correction. The response should be proportional to the evidence rather than driven by the embarrassment of admitting an error. Changing one's view can demonstrate intellectual integrity when it follows a defensible process of evaluation and leads to a better-supported understanding.
Study instruction:
- Practice documenting a belief revision by identifying the initial view, the new evidence, the evaluation process, and the revised conclusion.
Key point:
- Contradictory evidence should prompt investigation before acceptance or dismissal.
- Revising a belief is justified when better evidence warrants a different conclusion.
47. Q: How can managers evaluate whether a new approach is genuinely better than an existing one?
A: Show answer
Managers should define the objective and identify the performance measures that matter before comparing alternatives. Depending on the problem, these could include cost, quality, speed, customer satisfaction, error rates, employee workload, or long-term sustainability. When feasible, a pilot can compare the new approach with the existing process under reasonably comparable conditions. The analysis should account for implementation costs, time needed to learn the new method, and any unintended consequences. Managers should also identify who might benefit or lose from the change, because aggregate results can conceal important differences. A new approach should not be declared better solely because it is innovative or because its initial results appear positive. Evaluation must consider the original objective, the reliability of the evidence, and whether the improvement can be sustained.
Study instruction:
- Develop a comparison framework for an existing and a proposed process, including baseline performance, evaluation measures, implementation cost, and review timing.
Key point:
- Comparisons need clear objectives and relevant performance measures.
- Initial improvements should be examined alongside costs, limitations, and longer-term effects.
48. Q: How can people distinguish an evidence-based change of mind from indecision or inconsistency?
A: Show answer
An evidence-based change of mind occurs when a person revises a conclusion because relevant information, conditions, or reasoning has changed. Indecision, by contrast, may involve repeatedly changing direction without obtaining new evidence or applying stable decision criteria. Inconsistency can also arise when different standards are applied to similar situations without a defensible reason. To distinguish them, examine the original reasoning, the new information, and the explanation for the change. A person who documents why a previous assumption failed and what evidence supports the new approach demonstrates a learning process. A person who changes opinions merely to satisfy a different audience may be engaging in approval-seeking rather than genuine reconsideration. The goal is not permanent commitment or constant revision, but a transparent relationship between evidence and conclusions.
Study instruction:
- Compare one justified decision revision with one example of unstable decision-making and identify the role of evidence in each.
Key point:
- Good rethinking is driven by evidence and reasoned reassessment.
- Consistency in method matters more than refusing to change a conclusion.
49. Q: How should people respond when colleagues refuse to reconsider a strongly held position?
A: Show answer
A productive response begins by understanding what the colleague believes is at stake. Resistance may involve uncertainty, identity, professional reputation, previous investments, or concern about losing control. Simply presenting more arguments may reinforce defensiveness if the person interprets the discussion as a contest. Instead, ask which assumptions support the current position, acknowledge legitimate concerns, and explore what evidence would help distinguish the available options. A small, low-risk experiment may provide a more constructive basis for discussion than an abstract argument. Where the decision affects safety, compliance, or material resources, formal review and escalation may still be necessary. The objective is to improve the decision process without assuming that persuasion alone will change someone's mind or that every disagreement must end in consensus.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a discussion plan that identifies the colleague's concerns, the assumptions in dispute, the available evidence, and a possible low-risk test.
Key point:
- Understanding resistance can improve the way a challenge is presented.
- Evidence-based discussion may be more productive than escalating a personal argument.
50. Q: How can organizations build a lasting habit of rethinking into their decision-making processes?
A: Show answer
Organizations can make rethinking part of routine management rather than relying on occasional individual initiative. Important proposals can include explicit assumptions, alternative explanations, expected outcomes, review dates, and evidence that would justify changing course. Project reviews can compare predictions with actual results, while post-implementation assessments can document what worked, what failed, and what should change. Independent reviewers can challenge high-impact decisions, and teams can maintain records of recurring problems and unresolved risks. Leaders should also distinguish honest, well-supported revisions from careless decisions or repeated failures to follow established controls. A useful culture rewards accurate reporting and responsible learning, not simply confidence or rapid agreement. The objective is to ensure that new evidence can influence action even when it challenges established plans or senior opinions.
Study instruction:
- Draft a decision-review checklist that includes assumptions, alternatives, success measures, review dates, evidence of outcomes, and criteria for revision.
Key point:
- Reconsideration becomes more reliable when it is built into routine processes.
- Documented review helps organizations learn systematically from decisions and outcomes.
51. Q: What practical routine can individuals use to improve their ability to rethink?
A: Show answer
A practical routine begins by identifying an assumption that influences a meaningful decision. The individual then records the current conclusion and the evidence supporting it, followed by the strongest plausible alternative explanations. Next, they identify missing information and determine whether a small test, independent review, or targeted research could reduce uncertainty. After gathering evidence, they assess whether the original view remains justified and document any necessary revision. Finally, they consider what the experience reveals about the way the decision was made and whether future decisions need a different method. This routine can be applied to professional projects, personal commitments, financial choices, and relationships. It does not require questioning every minor belief constantly. It focuses attention on consequential assumptions where the cost of an error justifies additional examination.
Study instruction:
- Apply the routine to one important assumption this week and document the original belief, alternative explanations, evidence collected, and final assessment.
Key point:
- A repeatable routine turns rethinking into a practical skill.
- Focus on important assumptions where further evidence can improve the decision.
